### Executive Order 14292: Improving the Safety and Security of Biological Research

#### Issue, adoption and status

- **Executive Order 14292, Improving the Safety and Security of Biological Research, is dated 5 May 2025 in the Federal Register print of the order.** `ab-scr-eo14292-signed`
  2025-05-05 · signed
  _Date:_ Date in the heading of the printed order; published in the Federal Register of 8 May 2025 (90 FR 19611), which is a different date and is recorded in the document entry.
  _Limits:_ Records the order's date as printed. Signature is not implementation: nothing here establishes that any step the order directs was taken. The order's gain-of-function research provisions are outside this pass.
  > “Executive Order 14292 of May 5, 2025”  
  > [Executive Order 14292 of May 5, 2025: Improving the Safety and Security of Biological Research (90 FR 19611)](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2025-05-08/pdf/2025-08266.pdf), Heading, 90 FR 19611

#### What the text states

- **Section 4(b) of Executive Order 14292 directs the Director of the Office of Science and Technology Policy, within 90 days, to revise or replace the 2024 Framework for Nucleic Acid Synthesis Screening, directs the heads of all agencies that fund life-science research to ensure that synthetic nucleic acid procurement is conducted through providers or manufacturers that adhere to the updated framework, and states that the framework shall be reviewed and revised at least every four years.** `ab-scr-eo14292-sec4b-revise-framework`
  2025-05-08 · published
  _Date:_ Provision as the order states it, dated by the Federal Register publication of 8 May 2025; the order is dated 5 May 2025.
  _Limits:_ States what the order directs. It is not evidence that a revised or replacement framework was produced; the order also directs that the updated framework incorporate the enforcement mechanisms of its section 7, which are not recorded here. The order's characterisation of the approach it requires is its own wording.

  > “to ensure it takes a commonsense approach and effectively encourages providers of synthetic nucleic acid sequences to implement comprehensive, scalable, and verifiable synthetic nucleic acid procurement screening mechanisms to minimize the risk of misuse”  
  > [Executive Order 14292 of May 5, 2025: Improving the Safety and Security of Biological Research (90 FR 19611)](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2025-05-08/pdf/2025-08266.pdf), Sec. 4(b), 90 FR 19612
  > “The heads of all agencies that fund life-science research shall ensure that synthetic nucleic acid procurement is conducted through providers or manufacturers that adhere to the updated Framework.”  
  > [Executive Order 14292 of May 5, 2025: Improving the Safety and Security of Biological Research (90 FR 19611)](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2025-05-08/pdf/2025-08266.pdf), Sec. 4(b), 90 FR 19612
  > “The Framework shall be reviewed and revised at least every 4 years, or as appropriate.”  
  > [Executive Order 14292 of May 5, 2025: Improving the Safety and Security of Biological Research (90 FR 19611)](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2025-05-08/pdf/2025-08266.pdf), Sec. 4(b), final sentence, 90 FR 19612

- **Section 5 of Executive Order 14292 directs the Director of the Office of Science and Technology Policy, within 180 days, to develop and implement a strategy whose actions include achieving comprehensive, scalable and verifiable nucleic acid synthesis screening in non-federally funded settings, and states that any gaps in authorities needed for the strategy shall be addressed in a legislative proposal sent to the President within 180 days.** `ab-scr-eo14292-sec5-non-federal-screening`
  2025-05-08 · published
  _Date:_ Provision as the order states it, dated by the Federal Register publication of 8 May 2025; the order is dated 5 May 2025.
  _Limits:_ States what the order directs. No strategy and no legislative proposal is recorded as having been produced or sent; the section's wider subject is research that occurs without federal funding, which this record does not otherwise address.
  > “This strategy shall include actions to achieve comprehensive, scalable, and verifiable nucleic acid synthesis screening in non-federally funded settings.”  
  > [Executive Order 14292 of May 5, 2025: Improving the Safety and Security of Biological Research (90 FR 19611)](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2025-05-08/pdf/2025-08266.pdf), Sec. 5, 90 FR 19612
  > any gaps in the authorities needed to achieve the strategy's goals are to be addressed in a legislative proposal sent to the President, through the Director of OSTP and the APNSA, within 180 days of the order. _(paraphrase)_  
  > [Executive Order 14292 of May 5, 2025: Improving the Safety and Security of Biological Research (90 FR 19611)](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2025-05-08/pdf/2025-08266.pdf), Sec. 5, final sentence, 90 FR 19612

### IGSC Harmonized Screening Protocol v3.1

#### Issue, adoption and status

- **The International Gene Synthesis Consortium published version 3.1 of its Harmonized Screening Protocol, dated 1 June 2026, which states that it sets out the standards and practices that the consortium's provider and manufacturer members apply to prevent the misuse of synthetic nucleic acids.** `ab-scr-igsc-hsp-v31-published`
  2026-06-01 · published
  _Date:_ Date on the protocol's first page ('1 June, 2026') and in its own revision table.
  _Limits:_ Records the publication and the protocol's own description of itself. Publication is not evidence that any member applied any practice in it, and the consortium's statement that its members represent a majority of global commercial capacity is not recorded.
  > “This document outlines the standards and practices that International Gene Synthesis Consortium (IGSC) Provider and Manufacturer members apply to prevent the misuse of synthetic nucleic acids.”  
  > [IGSC Harmonized Screening Protocol v3.1](https://genesynthesisconsortium.org/wp-content/uploads/IGSC-Harmonized-Screening-Protocol-V3.1.pdf), 1 Preamble, p. 1
  > “Harmonized Screening Protocol v3.1”  
  > [IGSC Harmonized Screening Protocol v3.1](https://genesynthesisconsortium.org/wp-content/uploads/IGSC-Harmonized-Screening-Protocol-V3.1.pdf), First page, title and date

#### What the text states

- **Version 3.1 of the Harmonized Screening Protocol states its own revision history as five versions: v1.0 released on 18 November 2009, v2.0 on 19 November 2017, v2.1 on 5 May 2020, v3.0 on 3 September 2024 and v3.1 on 1 June 2026.** `ab-scr-igsc-hsp-revision-history`
  2026-06-01 · published
  _Date:_ The history as stated in the expression published on 1 June 2026. Each listed release date is the protocol's own statement about an earlier version; none of those earlier versions was retrieved.
  _Limits:_ The publisher's own account of its version history, recorded as such. The earlier versions were not retrieved and their texts are not recorded, so no change between versions is established here beyond what the table states. The table is paraphrased and not reproduced.

  > the revision table gives five rows: v1.0, 18 November 2009, initial publication; v2.0, 19 November 2017, a change to the update frequency of the consortium's screening database; v2.1, 5 May 2020, an added recommendation on screening pools of short fragments; v3.0, 3 September 2024, a rewrite to align with the 2023 United States guidance, Executive Order 14110 and the OSTP framework and to add context; v3.1, 1 June 2026, adding a requirement to validate the efficacy of screening systems regularly. _(paraphrase)_  
  > [IGSC Harmonized Screening Protocol v3.1](https://genesynthesisconsortium.org/wp-content/uploads/IGSC-Harmonized-Screening-Protocol-V3.1.pdf), 8 Revisions to the Harmonized Screening Protocol, p. 16

- **Version 3.1 of the Harmonized Screening Protocol states that members screen potential customers against the sanctions and denied-party lists of the countries relevant to their operations, require additional customer screening before accepting orders for sequences from regulated pathogens or toxins, and supply such sequences only to researchers in bona fide government laboratories, universities, non-profit research institutions or industrial laboratories demonstrably engaged in legitimate research.** `ab-scr-igsc-hsp-v31-customer-screening`
  2026-06-01 · published
  _Date:_ Provision as version 3.1 states it, dated by its publication.
  _Limits:_ States what the protocol says its members do and require. It is not evidence that any member screened any customer or refused any order, and membership is not compliance. The protocol's customer-screening appendix is not recorded.

  > “IGSC members supply nucleic acid sequences from regulated pathogens and toxins only to researchers in bona fide government laboratories, universities, non-profit research institutions, or industrial laboratories demonstrably engaged in legitimate research.”  
  > [IGSC Harmonized Screening Protocol v3.1](https://genesynthesisconsortium.org/wp-content/uploads/IGSC-Harmonized-Screening-Protocol-V3.1.pdf), 7.1 Customer Screening, requirement 1.3, p. 14
  > potential customers are subject to restricted-party screening against the relevant sanctions or denied-party lists maintained by the countries relevant to each provider's base of operations, and additional customer screening is required before accepting orders for nucleic acid sequences from regulated pathogens or toxins. _(paraphrase)_  
  > [IGSC Harmonized Screening Protocol v3.1](https://genesynthesisconsortium.org/wp-content/uploads/IGSC-Harmonized-Screening-Protocol-V3.1.pdf), 7.1 Customer Screening, requirements 1.2 and 1.3, p. 14

- **Version 3.1 of the Harmonized Screening Protocol states that members retain records of every gene synthesised and delivered for a minimum of eight years after shipping, including the recipient's identity and shipping address, and retain every sequence screening result for at least eight years.** `ab-scr-igsc-hsp-v31-record-retention`
  2026-06-01 · published
  _Date:_ Provision as version 3.1 states it, dated by its publication.
  _Limits:_ States a retention period the protocol sets. It is not evidence that any member holds such records. The 2023 United States guidance recommends at least three years; the two periods are different documents' provisions and are not compared here.

  > “IGSC members retain records of every gene synthesized and delivered for a minimum of 8 years after shipping”  
  > [IGSC Harmonized Screening Protocol v3.1](https://genesynthesisconsortium.org/wp-content/uploads/IGSC-Harmonized-Screening-Protocol-V3.1.pdf), 7.3 Record Keeping and Retention, requirement 3.1, p. 15
  > “IGSC members retain records of every gene sequence screening result for at least 8 years.”  
  > [IGSC Harmonized Screening Protocol v3.1](https://genesynthesisconsortium.org/wp-content/uploads/IGSC-Harmonized-Screening-Protocol-V3.1.pdf), 7.3 Record Keeping and Retention, requirement 3.2, p. 15

- **Version 3.1 of the Harmonized Screening Protocol states that members reserve the right to refuse to fill any order and to notify other members, law enforcement or other authorities on identifying potentially problematic orders, and that members will report requests from a suspicious potential customer or from one that fails to establish legitimacy under the protocol's customer-screening practices.** `ab-scr-igsc-hsp-v31-refusal-reporting`
  2026-06-01 · published
  _Date:_ Provision as version 3.1 states it, dated by its publication.
  _Limits:_ States what the protocol says members reserve the right to do and will do. No refusal, notification or report is recorded as having occurred, and the authorities named in the protocol are not listed here.

  > “IGSC members reserve the right to refuse to fill any order and to notify other IGSC members and/or law enforcement or other authorities upon identifying potentially problematic orders.”  
  > [IGSC Harmonized Screening Protocol v3.1](https://genesynthesisconsortium.org/wp-content/uploads/IGSC-Harmonized-Screening-Protocol-V3.1.pdf), 7.4 Order Refusal and Reporting, requirement 4.1, p. 15
  > members will report any request, from a suspicious potential customer or from a potential customer failing to establish legitimacy under the protocol's customer-screening practices, for a gene associated with an organism's pathogenicity. _(paraphrase)_

### Synthetic Nucleic Acid Screening pages (ASPR, Science, Safety and Security)

#### Issue, adoption and status

- **Within the pages and documents inspected on 28 October 2026, no revised or replacement Framework for Nucleic Acid Synthesis Screening was found: the Administration for Strategic Preparedness and Response's pages still link the 2024 framework and state that they will be updated once the revised framework is available.** `ab-scr-ostp-framework-replacement-not-found`
  2026-10-28 · undefined · Inferred
  _Date:_ Bounded by the check date and by the addresses inspected.
  _Reasoning:_ The inspected scope was: the ASPR synthetic nucleic acid screening hub page and its OSTP framework page (both retrieved 2026-10-28), the framework copy they link in the Biden White House archive, and a search of the Federal Register API for documents whose text contains "nucleic acid synthesis", "synthesis screening" or "Framework for Nucleic Acid Synthesis Screening" (17, 5 and 4 results, the latest being Executive Order 14292 of 2025). The only framework document located is the 2024 one marked as revised in September 2024, and the ASPR pages state that a revision is still awaited. No search was made of OSTP's own current website or of agency award terms, so this record says nothing about those.
  _Limits:_ A recorded absence within the stated scope at the stated date, not a finding that no revised framework exists or that none was issued. The 90-day deadline in Executive Order 14292 fell in 2025; whether anything was issued and withdrawn, or issued elsewhere, was not established. The provision kind is `threshold`, the kind the dataset uses for a stated date of effect or application (`omni-entry-into-force`, `ny-effective-date`); the schema has no `date` kind.
  > “This page will be updated once the new framework is available.”  
  > [OSTP Framework for Nucleic Acid Synthesis Screening (ASPR page)](https://www.aspr.gov/s3/synthetic-nucleic-acid-screening/ostp-framework-nucleic-acid-synthesis-screening), Update notice at the head of the page
  > the hub page states that OSTP issued the framework in 2024 and that the Executive Order of 5 May 2025 instructed federal departments and agencies to revise or replace it. _(paraphrase)_  
  > [Synthetic Nucleic Acid Screening (ASPR, Science, Safety and Security pages)](https://www.aspr.gov/s3/synthetic-nucleic-acid-screening), Body, closing paragraph · context

The two pages word the second sentence differently ('the revised framework' on the hub page, 'the new framework' on the framework page); each page is cited for its own wording and no significance is given to the difference. ### UK Biological Security Strategy

#### What the text states

- **The UK Biological Security Strategy of 12 June 2023 states that the convergence of bioscience with robotics, machine learning and AI has paved the way for automated approaches to biology, creating new cyberbiosecurity risk.** `ab-gov-uk-bss-2023-ai-convergence`
  2023-06-12 · published
  _Date:_ Dated by the strategy's publication on GOV.UK.
  _Limits:_ The strategy's own framing, in a passage about advances in bioscience; it is not a finding about any AI system and sets no requirement. The strategy's commitments on synthetic nucleic acid screening are recorded in this pack's synthesis screening strand, not here.
  > “The convergence of bioscience with robotics, machine learning and AI has paved the way for automated approaches to biology”  
  > [UK Biological Security Strategy (HTML edition on GOV.UK)](https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/uk-biological-security-strategy/uk-biological-security-strategy-html), Chapter on the opportunities and risks of advances in bioscience and biotechnology (paragraph on DNA synthesis capabilities and convergence)

### Executive Order 14110: Safe, Secure, and Trustworthy Development and Use of Artificial Intelligence

#### What the text states

- **Section 4.2(a)(i)(C) of Executive Order 14110 provides that, before NIST develops red-team testing standards, the description companies report shall include the results of any red-team testing the company has conducted relating to lowering the barrier to entry for the development, acquisition and use of biological weapons by non-state actors.** `ab-gov-us-eo14110-sec42ai-bio-redteam`
  2023-10-30 · signed · adds to dataset record `fl-us-eo14110-sec42a-reporting`
  _Date:_ Dated by the order's signature.
  _Limits:_ Adds the biological element of the reporting duty already recorded in the dataset. The order directs the Secretary of Commerce to require the reporting; it does not itself impose the duty, and no company submission is recorded. Executive Order 14110 was revoked by Executive Order 14148 of 20 January 2025 (dataset record fl-us-eo14110-revoked).

  > “the results of any red-team testing that the company has conducted relating to lowering the barrier to entry for the development, acquisition, and use of biological weapons by non-state actors”  
  > [Executive Order 14110 of October 30, 2023: Safe, Secure, and Trustworthy Development and Use of Artificial Intelligence (88 FR 75191)](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2023-11-01/pdf/2023-24283.pdf), Sec. 4.2(a)(i)(C), 88 FR 75197

- **Section 4.4 of Executive Order 14110, headed "Reducing Risks at the Intersection of AI and CBRN Threats", states that the actions it directs are to better understand and mitigate the risk of AI being misused to assist in the development or use of CBRN threats, with a particular focus on biological weapons.** `ab-gov-us-eo14110-sec44-purpose`
  2023-10-30 · signed
  _Date:_ Dated by the order's signature; the subsections it introduces carry their own deadlines.
  _Limits:_ States the order's own purpose for this section. It is not evidence that any action was taken under it, and it names no AI system. Executive Order 14110 was revoked by Executive Order 14148 of 20 January 2025 (dataset record fl-us-eo14110-revoked); the revocation does not erase the text recorded here.

  > “To better understand and mitigate the risk of AI being misused to assist in the development or use of CBRN threats—with a particular focus on biological weapons”  
  > [Executive Order 14110 of October 30, 2023: Safe, Secure, and Trustworthy Development and Use of Artificial Intelligence (88 FR 75191)](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2023-11-01/pdf/2023-24283.pdf), Sec. 4.4(a), 88 FR 75200

- **Section 4.4(a)(i) of Executive Order 14110 directs the Secretary of Homeland Security, within 180 days and in consultation with the Secretary of Energy and the Director of OSTP, to evaluate the potential for AI to be misused to enable the development or production of CBRN threats while also considering AI's benefits and application in countering them, consulting experts from the Department of Energy, private AI laboratories, academia and third-party model evaluators.** `ab-gov-us-eo14110-sec44ai-dhs-evaluation`
  2023-10-30 · signed
  _Date:_ Dated by the order's signature; the directive runs 180 days from that date.
  _Limits:_ A directive to an agency head. No record is made that the evaluation was carried out, and the order names no AI laboratory or evaluator. The same subsection requires a report to the President with recommendations, which is not separately recorded in this pass. Executive Order 14110 was revoked by Executive Order 14148 of 20 January 2025 (dataset record fl-us-eo14110-revoked).

  > “shall evaluate the potential for AI to be misused to enable the development or production of CBRN threats, while also considering the benefits and application of AI to counter these threats”  
  > [Executive Order 14110 of October 30, 2023: Safe, Secure, and Trustworthy Development and Use of Artificial Intelligence (88 FR 75191)](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2023-11-01/pdf/2023-24283.pdf), Sec. 4.4(a)(i), 88 FR 75200
  > “consult with experts in AI and CBRN issues from the Department of Energy, private AI laboratories, academia, and third-party model evaluators”  
  > [Executive Order 14110 of October 30, 2023: Safe, Secure, and Trustworthy Development and Use of Artificial Intelligence (88 FR 75191)](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2023-11-01/pdf/2023-24283.pdf), Sec. 4.4(a)(i)(A), 88 FR 75200-75201

- **Section 4.4(a)(ii) of Executive Order 14110 directs the Secretary of Defense, within 120 days, to contract with the National Academies of Sciences, Engineering, and Medicine for a study that assesses the ways in which AI can increase biosecurity risks, including risks from generative AI models trained on biological data, and that makes recommendations on mitigating them.** `ab-gov-us-eo14110-sec44aii-academies-study`
  2023-10-30 · signed

The subsection's further elements, including the national security implications of government-held datasets, are summarised only in the statement's first clause and are not separately recorded.

  > “assesses the ways in which AI can increase biosecurity risks, including risks from generative AI models trained on biological data, and makes recommendations on how to mitigate these risks”

- **Section 4.1(b) of Executive Order 14110 directs the Secretary of Energy, within 270 days, to develop and, to the extent permitted by law and available appropriations, implement a plan for developing the Department of Energy's AI model evaluation tools and AI testbeds, and provides that the Secretary shall at a minimum develop tools to evaluate AI capabilities to generate outputs that may represent nuclear, nonproliferation, biological, chemical, critical infrastructure and energy-security threats or hazards, doing this work solely to guard against those threats and also developing model guardrails that reduce such risks.** `ab-gov-us-eo14110-sec41b-doe-tools`

### Regulation (EU) 2024/1689 (Artificial Intelligence Act), as published in the Official Journal

#### Issue, adoption and status

- **Within the inspected scope, a case-insensitive search of the Official Journal English text of Regulation (EU) 2024/1689, as cached on 28 October 2026, finds no occurrence of 'CBRN', 'biosecurity', 'nucleic' or 'pathogen' and only two occurrences of 'biological', both outside the articles: recital 110 and point (d) of Annex XIII.** `ab-gov-eu-ai-act-no-article-cbrn`
  2024-07-12 · published · Inferred
  _Date:_ Absence recorded for the Official Journal expression of 12 July 2024; amendments made after that date, including Regulation (EU) 2026/1744, were not searched.
  _Reasoning:_ Premises: the inspected scope was the English Official Journal text of Regulation (EU) 2024/1689 (recitals, articles and annexes) as retrieved from the Publications Office repository on 2026-10-28, 90,004 words. Method: case-insensitive counts of the terms named in the statement ('biological' 2, 'chemical' 1, 'CBRN' 0, 'nucleic' 0, 'pathogen' 0, 'biosecurity' 0) and reading of the context of each occurrence of 'biological', which fall in recital 110 and in Annex XIII, point (d). The record means not found within that scope; it says nothing about the Code of Practice, Commission guidelines or amending acts, which are recorded separately.
  _Limits:_ A bounded string search of one expression, not an interpretation of the Regulation's scope: provisions on systemic risk that do not use these words may still cover such risk, and the Code of Practice's treatment of CBRN risk is recorded separately.
  > 'biological' occurs twice, in recital 110 and in Annex XIII, point (d); 'chemical' once, in recital 110; 'CBRN', 'biosecurity', 'nucleic' and 'pathogen' do not occur. _(paraphrase)_  
  > [Regulation (EU) 2024/1689 (Artificial Intelligence Act), Official Journal L series 2024/1689 of 12.7.2024, English XHTML manifestation from the Publications Office repository (Cellar)](http://publications.europa.eu/resource/cellar/dc8116a1-3fe6-11ef-865a-01aa75ed71a1.0006.03/DOC_1), Whole cached text (OJ L 2024/1689)

#### What the text states

- **Point (d) of Annex XIII to Regulation (EU) 2024/1689, which gives the criteria for the designation of general-purpose AI models with systemic risk referred to in Article 51 and which the Commission takes into account for the purpose of determining that a model has capabilities or an impact equivalent to those set out in Article 51(1), point (a), names the model's input and output modalities, the state of the art thresholds for determining high-impact capabilities for each modality, and the specific type of inputs and outputs, giving biological sequences as the example of that type.** `ab-gov-eu-ai-act-annex13-biological-sequences`
  2024-07-12 · published · adds to dataset record `fl-eu-aia-annex13-criteria`
  _Date:_ Provision as published in the Official Journal on 12 July 2024. Annex XIII is applied through Article 51; the application dates are recorded in the dataset.
  _Limits:_ Adds the biological example in point (d) to the dataset's record of the Annex XIII criteria. It sets no threshold for biological-sequence models and does not say that any model falls within the Annex; whether any provider is covered is legal application and is not recorded. The Annex is a list of criteria for a designation decision, not a provision addressed to providers.

  > “and the specific type of inputs and outputs (e.g. biological sequences)”  
  > [Regulation (EU) 2024/1689 (Artificial Intelligence Act), Official Journal L series 2024/1689 of 12.7.2024, English XHTML manifestation from the Publications Office repository (Cellar)](http://publications.europa.eu/resource/cellar/dc8116a1-3fe6-11ef-865a-01aa75ed71a1.0006.03/DOC_1), Annex XIII, point (d)
  > “Criteria for the designation of general-purpose AI models with systemic risk referred to in Article 51 … the Commission shall take into account the following criteria”  
  > [Regulation (EU) 2024/1689 (Artificial Intelligence Act), Official Journal L series 2024/1689 of 12.7.2024, English XHTML manifestation from the Publications Office repository (Cellar)](http://publications.europa.eu/resource/cellar/dc8116a1-3fe6-11ef-865a-01aa75ed71a1.0006.03/DOC_1), Annex XIII, heading and opening words · context

- **Recital 110 of Regulation (EU) 2024/1689 states that international approaches have so far identified the need to pay attention to, among other risks of general-purpose AI models, chemical, biological, radiological and nuclear risks, such as the ways in which barriers to entry can be lowered, including for weapons development, design acquisition or use.** `ab-gov-eu-ai-act-recital110-cbrn`
  2024-07-12 · published
  _Date:_ Provision as published in the Official Journal on 12 July 2024; the Regulation is dated 13 June 2024. A recital states reasoning and is not itself an obligation.
  _Limits:_ A recital, which explains the Regulation rather than imposing a duty; the Regulation's articles are not recorded as naming this risk (see ab-gov-eu-ai-act-no-article-cbrn). It is not a finding that any model lowers any barrier, and it names no model or provider.
  > “chemical, biological, radiological, and nuclear risks, such as the ways in which barriers to entry can be lowered”  
  > [Regulation (EU) 2024/1689 (Artificial Intelligence Act), Official Journal L series 2024/1689 of 12.7.2024, English XHTML manifestation from the Publications Office repository (Cellar)](http://publications.europa.eu/resource/cellar/dc8116a1-3fe6-11ef-865a-01aa75ed71a1.0006.03/DOC_1), Recital 110

### Memorandum on Advancing the United States' Leadership in Artificial Intelligence; Harnessing Artificial Intelligence to Fulfill National Security Objectives; and Fostering the Safety, Security, and Trustworthiness of Artificial Intelligence

#### Issue, adoption and status

- **The White House issued the national security memorandum on advancing United States leadership in artificial intelligence, harnessing AI for national security objectives and fostering AI safety, security and trustworthiness, posted on its briefing-room page under the date 24 October 2024.** `ab-gov-us-nsm-ai-2024-issued`
  2024-10-24 · published
  _Date:_ The date line of the archived White House page. The cached text carries no signature line, no memorandum number and no Federal Register citation, so the date is the page's publication date, not a stated signature date.
  _Limits:_ Records the issuance of the memorandum as the archived page presents it. It is not evidence of implementation of any directive in it, and whether it was later revoked or superseded was not established in this pass. A classified annex referred to in Section 1(a) was not retrieved.
  > the archived White House briefing-room page is dated 24 October 2024 and carries the memorandum's distribution list and subject block, with the subject "Advancing the United States' Leadership in Artificial Intelligence; Harnessing Artificial Intelligence to Fulfill National Security Objectives; and Fostering the Safety, Security, and Trustworthiness of Artificial Intelligence". _(paraphrase)_  
  > [Memorandum on Advancing the United States' Leadership in Artificial Intelligence; Harnessing Artificial Intelligence to Fulfill National Security Objectives; and Fostering the Safety, Security, and Trustworthiness of Artificial Intelligence](https://bidenwhitehouse.archives.gov/briefing-room/presidential-actions/2024/10/24/memorandum-on-advancing-the-united-states-leadership-in-artificial-intelligence-harnessing-artificial-intelligence-to-fulfill-national-security-objectives-and-fostering-the-safety-security/), Page header and date line; subject block of the memorandum

#### What the text states

- **Subsection 3.3(e)(ii) of the national security memorandum of 24 October 2024 directs the AI Safety Institute, within 180 days, to issue guidance for AI developers on testing, evaluating and managing risks from dual-use foundation models, with the first listed topic being how to measure capabilities relevant to the risk that AI models could enable the development of biological and chemical weapons or the automation of offensive cyber operations.** `ab-gov-us-nsm-aisi-guidance-bio-chem`
  2024-10-24 · published
  _Date:_ Dated by the memorandum's posting; the directive runs 180 days from that date.
  _Limits:_ A directive naming a guidance topic. It states no method and no measurement result. The guidance published as NIST AI 800-1 drafts is recorded separately in the dataset; this record does not assert that those drafts discharge this directive.

  > “How to measure capabilities that are relevant to the risk that AI models could enable the development of biological and chemical weapons or the automation of offensive cyber operations”  
  > [Memorandum on Advancing the United States' Leadership in Artificial Intelligence; Harnessing Artificial Intelligence to Fulfill National Security Objectives; and Fostering the Safety, Security, and Trustworthiness of Artificial Intelligence](https://bidenwhitehouse.archives.gov/briefing-room/presidential-actions/2024/10/24/memorandum-on-advancing-the-united-states-leadership-in-artificial-intelligence-harnessing-artificial-intelligence-to-fulfill-national-security-objectives-and-fostering-the-safety-security/), Subsection 3.3(e)(ii)(A)

- **Subsection 3.3(e)(i) of the national security memorandum of 24 October 2024 directs the AI Safety Institute, within 180 days and subject to private sector cooperation, to pursue voluntary preliminary testing of at least two frontier AI models before their public deployment or release, and provides that the testing shall assess models' capabilities to aid offensive cyber operations, accelerate development of biological and/or chemical weapons, autonomously carry out malicious behaviour, automate development and deployment of other models with such capabilities, and give rise to other risks identified by the institute.** `ab-gov-us-nsm-aisi-two-models`
  2024-10-24 · published
  _Date:_ Dated by the memorandum's posting; the directive runs 180 days from that date.
  _Limits:_ A directive conditioned on private sector cooperation. It names no model and no developer, and it is not evidence that any testing was performed; evaluations actually published are recorded elsewhere in this pack's evaluations strand. The memorandum's list of capabilities to be assessed ends with risks identified by the institute, so it is not a closed list.

  > “AISI shall pursue voluntary preliminary testing of at least two frontier AI models prior to their public deployment or release”  
  > [Memorandum on Advancing the United States' Leadership in Artificial Intelligence; Harnessing Artificial Intelligence to Fulfill National Security Objectives; and Fostering the Safety, Security, and Trustworthiness of Artificial Intelligence](https://bidenwhitehouse.archives.gov/briefing-room/presidential-actions/2024/10/24/memorandum-on-advancing-the-united-states-leadership-in-artificial-intelligence-harnessing-artificial-intelligence-to-fulfill-national-security-objectives-and-fostering-the-safety-security/), Subsection 3.3(e)(i)
  > “This testing shall assess models' capabilities to aid offensive cyber operations, accelerate development of biological and/or chemical weapons, autonomously carry out malicious behavior, automate development and deployment of other models with such capabilities, and give rise to other risks identified by AISI”  
  > [Memorandum on Advancing the United States' Leadership in Artificial Intelligence; Harnessing Artificial Intelligence to Fulfill National Security Objectives; and Fostering the Safety, Security, and Trustworthiness of Artificial Intelligence](https://bidenwhitehouse.archives.gov/briefing-room/presidential-actions/2024/10/24/memorandum-on-advancing-the-united-states-leadership-in-artificial-intelligence-harnessing-artificial-intelligence-to-fulfill-national-security-objectives-and-fostering-the-safety-security/), Subsection 3.3(e)(i) (continued)

- **Subsection 3.3(c) of the national security memorandum of 24 October 2024 directs the Department of Commerce to establish an enduring capability to lead voluntary unclassified pre-deployment safety testing of frontier AI models on behalf of the United States Government, including assessments of risks relating to cybersecurity, biosecurity, chemical weapons, system autonomy and other risks as appropriate, with nuclear risk assessment led by the Department of Energy.** `ab-gov-us-nsm-aisi-unclassified-testing`
  2024-10-24 · published
  _Date:_ Dated by the memorandum's posting; the subsection states no deadline for this directive.
  _Limits:_ A directive, and testing that the memorandum itself describes as voluntary. It is not evidence that any capability was established, that any model was tested, or that any developer took part.

  > “Commerce shall establish an enduring capability to lead voluntary unclassified pre-deployment safety testing of frontier AI models on behalf of the United States Government, including assessments of risks relating to cybersecurity, biosecurity, chemical weapons, system autonomy, and other risks as appropriate”  
  > [Memorandum on Advancing the United States' Leadership in Artificial Intelligence; Harnessing Artificial Intelligence to Fulfill National Security Objectives; and Fostering the Safety, Security, and Trustworthiness of Artificial Intelligence](https://bidenwhitehouse.archives.gov/briefing-room/presidential-actions/2024/10/24/memorandum-on-advancing-the-united-states-leadership-in-artificial-intelligence-harnessing-artificial-intelligence-to-fulfill-national-security-objectives-and-fostering-the-safety-security/), Subsection 3.3(c)

- **Subsection 3.3(g)(i) of the national security memorandum of 24 October 2024 states that the United States Government shall advance classified evaluations of advanced AI models' capacity to generate or exacerbate deliberate chemical and biological threats, and directs the Department of Energy, the Department of Homeland Security and the AI Safety Institute, within 210 days, to develop a roadmap for such evaluations that considers their scope, scale and priority and proper safeguards to ensure that evaluations and simulations are not misconstrued as offensive capability development.** `ab-gov-us-nsm-classified-evaluations`
  2024-10-24 · published

A further element of the same subsection directs a Department of Energy pilot project within 270 days; it is not separately recorded.

### NIST AI 800-1 (second public draft): Managing Misuse Risk for Dual-Use Foundation Models

#### What the text states

- **The second public draft of NIST AI 800-1 states that its Appendix D sets out additional considerations relevant to identifying, measuring and mitigating risks associated with the misuse of foundation models for the development or use of dangerous biological or chemical agents, and that its recommendations are primarily relevant for foundation model developers and deployers and for third-party evaluators working with them.** `ab-gov-us-nist-800-1-2pd-appendix-d`
  2025-01-15 · published · adds to dataset record `fl-us-nist-ai-800-1-2pd-changes`
  _Date:_ Dated by the publication of the second public draft, as recorded in the dataset claim fl-us-nist-ai-800-1-2pd-published.
  _Limits:_ Records the appendix's own statement of its scope and audience, in a draft for comment. It is not guidance that anyone is recorded as following, and no content of the appendix beyond this scope statement is recorded.
  > “additional considerations relevant to identifying, measuring, and mitigating risks associated with the misuse of foundation models for the development or use of dangerous biological or chemical agents”  
  > [NIST AI 800-1 2pd (Second Public Draft): Managing Misuse Risk for Dual-Use Foundation Models](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/NIST.AI.800-1.ipd2.pdf), Appendix D, opening paragraph, PDF p. 36 (draft p. 31)

### General-Purpose AI Code of Practice (final version, 10 July 2025)

#### What the text states

- **Appendix 1.3.1 of the Safety and Security chapter of the General-Purpose AI Code of Practice lists, among the model capabilities treated as non-exhaustive potential sources of systemic risk for systemic risk identification, chemical, biological, radiological and nuclear (CBRN) capabilities and other such weapon acquisition or proliferation capabilities.** `ab-gov-eu-cop-cbrn-capability-source`
  2025-07-10 · published
  _Date:_ Dated by the publication of the final Code.
  _Limits:_ What the Code's text says, in a voluntary instrument. It is not evidence that any signatory identified such a capability in any model, and the list is stated to be non-exhaustive.

  > “Model capabilities include: (1) offensive cyber capabilities; (2) Chemical, Biological, Radiological, and Nuclear (CBRN) capabilities, and other such weapon acquisition or proliferation capabilities”  
  > [General-Purpose AI Code of Practice (final version, 10 July 2025): Safety and Security chapter](https://ec.europa.eu/newsroom/dae/redirection/document/118119), Appendix 1.3.1 (Model capabilities), PDF p. 35 of the file served on 2026-10-28

- **Appendix 1.4, point (1), of the Safety and Security chapter of the General-Purpose AI Code of Practice states that the specified systemic risk "chemical, biological, radiological and nuclear" means risks from enabling CBRN attacks or accidents, including significantly lowering the barriers to entry for malicious actors, or significantly increasing the potential impact achieved, in the design, development, acquisition, release, distribution and use of related weapons or materials.** `ab-gov-eu-cop-cbrn-risk-definition`
  2025-07-10 · published · adds to dataset record `prov-eu-code-ss-specified-systemic-risks`
  _Date:_ Dated by the publication of the final Code.
  _Limits:_ Adds the content of the first specified systemic risk to the dataset's record of the Appendix 1.4 list. It is a definition in a voluntary instrument, not a threshold, a test or a finding about any model, and it names no measure of "significantly".
  > “Chemical, biological, radiological and nuclear: Risks from enabling chemical, biological, radiological, and nuclear (CBRN) attacks or accidents. This includes significantly lowering the barriers to entry for malicious actors, or significantly increasing the potential impact achieved”  
  > [General-Purpose AI Code of Practice (final version, 10 July 2025): Safety and Security chapter](https://ec.europa.eu/newsroom/dae/redirection/document/118119), Appendix 1.4, point (1), PDF p. 37 of the file served on 2026-10-28

### Winning the Race: America's AI Action Plan

#### What the text states

- **America's AI Action Plan states, in its "Invest in Biosecurity" section, that while AI will unlock potential in biology it could create new pathways for malicious actors to synthesize harmful pathogens and other biomolecules, and that the solution is a multi-tiered approach to screen for malicious actors together with new tools and infrastructure for more effective screening.** `ab-gov-us-action-plan-biosecurity-section`
  2025-07-23 · published
  _Date:_ Dated by the plan's release, as recorded in the dataset claim fl-us-ai-action-plan-published.
  _Limits:_ The plan's own framing of a risk and of its preferred approach. It is not a finding about any AI system's capability, and the plan states no evidence for the risk in this section.

  > “it could create new pathways for malicious actors to synthesize harmful pathogens and other biomolecules”  
  > [Winning the Race: America's AI Action Plan (July 2025)](https://www.whitehouse.gov/wp-content/uploads/2025/07/Americas-AI-Action-Plan.pdf), Pillar III, "Invest in Biosecurity", opening paragraph, PDF p. 26 (printed p. 23)

- **America's AI Action Plan states, in the section on the United States Government being at the forefront of evaluating national security risks in frontier models, that the most powerful AI systems may pose novel national security risks in the near future in areas such as cyberattacks and the development of chemical, biological, radiological, nuclear or explosives (CBRNE) weapons.** `ab-gov-us-action-plan-cbrne-frontier-risk`
  2025-07-23 · published · adds to dataset record `fl-us-ai-action-plan-caisi-frontier-evaluations`
  _Date:_ Dated by the plan's release.
  _Limits:_ The plan's framing of possible future risk, in its own conditional wording. It is not a finding about any model's capability and names no system. The recommended CAISI-led evaluations in the same section are recorded in the dataset (fl-us-ai-action-plan-caisi-frontier-evaluations).

  > “The most powerful AI systems may pose novel national security risks in the near future in areas such as cyberattacks and the development of chemical, biological, radiological, nuclear, or explosives (CBRNE) weapons”  
  > [Winning the Race: America's AI Action Plan (July 2025)](https://www.whitehouse.gov/wp-content/uploads/2025/07/Americas-AI-Action-Plan.pdf), Pillar III, "Ensure that the U.S. Government is at the Forefront of Evaluating National Security Risks in Frontier Models", opening paragraph, PDF p. 25 (printed p. 22)

- **America's AI Action Plan recommends requiring all institutions that receive federal funding for scientific research to use nucleic acid synthesis tools and synthesis providers with robust nucleic acid sequence screening and customer verification procedures, and creating enforcement mechanisms for that requirement rather than relying on voluntary attestation.** `ab-gov-us-action-plan-federal-funding-screening`
  2025-07-23 · published
  _Date:_ Dated by the plan's release.
  _Limits:_ A recommended policy action in a plan, not a legal duty, a rule or an appropriation. It names no agency as the enforcer, no provider and no screening standard, and it is not evidence that any requirement was imposed. The 2024 OSTP framework and later screening actions are recorded in this pack's synthesis screening strand.

  > “Require all institutions receiving Federal funding for scientific research to use nucleic acid synthesis tools and synthesis providers that have robust nucleic acid sequence screening and customer verification procedures. Create enforcement mechanisms for this requirement rather than relying on voluntary attestation.”  
  > [Winning the Race: America's AI Action Plan (July 2025)](https://www.whitehouse.gov/wp-content/uploads/2025/07/Americas-AI-Action-Plan.pdf), Pillar III, "Invest in Biosecurity", Recommended Policy Actions, first bullet, PDF p. 26 (printed p. 23)

- **America's AI Action Plan recommends that, led by OSTP, government and industry actors be convened to develop a mechanism to facilitate data sharing between nucleic acid synthesis providers to screen for potentially fraudulent or malicious customers.** `ab-gov-us-action-plan-provider-data-sharing`

  > “highlighting the importance of stronger guard rails and AI governance”

### Statement by the United States (BWC/MSP/2025/WP.14)

#### What the text states

- **The statement by the United States issued as document BWC/MSP/2025/WP.14 at the 2025 Meeting of States Parties states that advanced and emerging technologies, including AI, can aid Biological Weapons Convention verification and implementation and help stop dangerous dual-use biological research, and calls on every State Party to join its efforts to seek ways in which AI and other tools can help verify Convention compliance, enhance transparency and strengthen the collective ability of States Parties to detect, defend against and respond to biological threats.** `ab-gov-bwc-us-wp14-ai-verification`
  2025-12-17 · published
  _Date:_ Date on the document; the 2025 Meeting of States Parties sat in Geneva on 15-17 December 2025. The document is marked as issued without formal editing.
  _Limits:_ A statement of one State Party's position, recorded as what the document says. It is not a decision of the Meeting, not a described method, and not evidence that AI aids verification, that any technology has been used for it, or that any State Party joined the call; the statement's assertions about other States Parties are not recorded. The call is addressed to other States Parties, which no instrument requires of them, so the provision kind is the risk and subject domain of the passage rather than a required action; whether states_provision suits a national statement at all is an open question for the maintainer.
  > “Advanced and emerging technologies, including AI, can aid BWC verification and implementation and help stop dangerous dual-use biological research.”  
  > [Statement by the United States (BWC/MSP/2025/WP.14)](https://documents.un.org/doc/undoc/gen/g25/211/02/pdf/g2521102.pdf), Paragraph 5
  > “we call on every State … Party to join us in our efforts to seek ways in which AI and other tools can help us verify BWC compliance, enhance transparency, and strengthen our collective ability to detect, defend against, and respond to biological threats”  
  > [Statement by the United States (BWC/MSP/2025/WP.14)](https://documents.un.org/doc/undoc/gen/g25/211/02/pdf/g2521102.pdf), Paragraph 5, across the page break (PDF pp. 1-2)

### Executive Order 14409: Promoting Advanced Artificial Intelligence Innovation and Security

#### Issue, adoption and status

- **Within the inspected scope, a case-insensitive search of the Federal Register text of Executive Order 14409 of 2 June 2026, as cached on 28 October 2026, finds no occurrence of 'biological', 'biosecurity', 'CBRN', 'chemical', 'pathogen', 'nucleic' or 'weapon'.** `ab-gov-us-eo14409-no-biological-provision`
  2026-06-02 · signed · Inferred
  _Date:_ Absence recorded for this expression and dated by the order's signature; the Federal Register print is of 5 June 2026.
  _Reasoning:_ Premises: the inspected scope was the Federal Register text of Executive Order 14409 (91 FR 34565-34567), as served by govinfo.gov on 2026-10-28 (whole text, 1,317 words of text layer). Method: case-insensitive search for each term named in the statement, each returning zero matches, and reading of the order's purpose and of Sections 2 and 3, whose provisions concern cyber defence of systems, an AI cybersecurity clearinghouse and a classified benchmarking process for advanced cyber capabilities. The record means not found within that scope; it says nothing about other orders, about measures taken under this one, or about any classified annex.
  _Limits:_ A bounded string search of one expression. It is not a claim that the administration took no action on AI and biological risk, and it does not characterise the order's purpose beyond what the text states.
  > the search for the seven terms returned no match; the order's risk vocabulary is cyber (cyber defence, software vulnerabilities, 'advanced cyber capabilities of AI models'), and Section 3(a) ties the 'covered frontier model' designation to a classified benchmarking process for advanced cyber capabilities. _(paraphrase)_

  > “人工智能大幅降低获取核生化导武器等高危领域专业知识门槛，辅以检索增强生成功能，如不能有效管控，可能被不法分子、极端势力、恐怖分子恶意利用” — _working translation:_ “Artificial intelligence substantially lowers the threshold for obtaining specialist knowledge in high-risk fields such as nuclear, biological, chemical and missile weapons; supplemented by retrieval-augmented generation functions, if it cannot be effectively controlled it may be maliciously used by criminals, extremist forces and terrorists.”

### Can large language models democratize access to dual-use biotechnology?

#### Issue, adoption and status

- **The preprint "Can large language models democratize access to dual-use biotechnology?" (arXiv v1, 6 June 2023) states that large language models will make pandemic-class agents widely accessible even to people with little or no laboratory training.** `ab-arg-b1-soice-articulates`

## Omissions

Listed with reasons. An omission is not a judgement of importance.

- **Urbina, Lentzos, Invernizzi and Ekins, "Dual use of artificial-intelligence-powered drug discovery" (Nature Machine Intelligence, 7 March 2022)** (arguments). Could not be retrieved: the PMC copy served a browser-check page and the Europe PMC full-text endpoint returned HTTP 500 (see retrievalFailures). It is an obvious earlier candidate for the design-tools proposition (B4) and for the premise that design tools favour skilled actors (B7); the earliest-located roles for both are therefore bounded by what could be retrieved.
- **OpenAI's own argument and early-warning posts on biological risk (January 2024 onward)** (arguments). openai.com refuses requests from this environment, per the task brief of 2026-10-28, and no record may rest on a text not retrieved. All of these texts postdate the texts marked earliest located here.
- **NTI's October 2023 report on the convergence of AI and biotechnology** (arguments). nti.org returned HTTP 403 and the Wayback capture of the same PDF also returned HTTP 403 (both observed 2026-10-28). The cross-family checker, who can reach the Archive, is asked to open the capture recorded in retrievalFailures.
- **Gopal and colleagues' 2023 preprint on fine-tuning and open weights (arXiv 2310.18233)** (arguments). Left out on hazard grounds: the proposition it would bear on (B6) is already carried by two texts whose argument-level statements can be recorded without approaching the paper's subject matter. An omission is not a judgement of importance.
- **Wet-lab and virology benchmark results, uplift trial scores and model-card evaluation findings** (arguments). These belong to the pack's `evaluations` pass. This pass records only what texts argue, so the Anthropic post's trial result, for example, is not recorded here even though its methodological statement is.
- **Testimony, commission reports, press commentary and forum posts** (arguments). Not searched systematically (see howWeSearched); several were visible in search results. None is recorded, so none supports an earliest-located role.
- **Texts on AI and chemical weapons risk** (arguments). Outside the pack's scope, which is biological risk, even where a text treats the two together.
- **OpenAI's own evaluation publications on biological risk, such as 'Building an early warning system for LLM-aided biological threat creation' (January 2024) and the June 2025 blog post the system cards refer to** (evaluations). openai.com returned HTTP 403 to this environment and the Internet Archive capture also refused; no PDF address on cdn.openai.com was found. Recorded as retrieval failures rather than from memory.
- **The Claude Opus 4 / Claude Sonnet 4 system card of May 2025, and Anthropic's ASL-3 Deployment Safeguards Report** (evaluations). Neither was linked from the pages retrieved under a stable name; the ASL-3 activation post and later cards were used instead. The Claude 4 card is the fuller source for the May 2025 determination and should be added if its address can be established.
- **Microsoft and other developers' model cards** (evaluations). No Microsoft model card stating a biological evaluation was located from the pages retrieved; Microsoft's framework edition belongs to the frameworks pass.
- **Non-US developers' model cards stating a biological evaluation** (evaluations). The Kimi-K2-Thinking card and the Qwen3 technical report abstract were checked and name no biological or CBRN evaluation (the Kimi reading is recorded as a bounded negative record). Other Chinese and non-US cards were not searched systematically; this is a gap, not a finding.
- **CAISI's published evaluation of DeepSeek models (September 2025)** (evaluations). The guessed NIST address returned HTTP 404 and no alternative was located in the time available. Recorded as a retrieval failure.
- **The detailed technical reports behind the two joint US AISI / UK AISI pre-deployment evaluations, and UK AISI's own reports on them** (evaluations). Only the NIST summary pages were retrieved; the linked technical reports were not. The published biological findings therefore rest on the summaries.
- **Passages in the Claude 3.7 Sonnet card and the ChatGPT Agent card describing where model performance improved within a weaponisation or threat-creation pathway** (evaluations). Hazard-safe register: these passages are technical content. Only determination-level and comparative statements are recorded.
- **The unlearning method and results of the WMDP paper, and the full bodies of both benchmark papers** (evaluations). Out of scope for an evaluation record and, for the paper bodies, deliberately not read under the hazard-safe rule; only the abstract pages were retrieved.
- **Gemini 2.5 and earlier Gemini model cards, and the Gemini 3 Pro Frontier Safety Framework report** (evaluations). storage.googleapis.com/model-cards returned HTTP 403, the deepmind.google pages render their document links in JavaScript, and the guessed address for the framework report returned HTTP 404.
- **UK AISI's report on Claude Sonnet 4.5 summarised in that card** (evaluations). The testing reported there concerns misalignment propensities, not biological capability, so it falls outside this pass.
- **Meta Frontier AI Framework v1.1 and Meta Advanced AI Scaling Framework v2: no record at all.** (frameworks). Neither edition could be retrieved. ai.meta.com answered HTTP 400 to every request from this environment, including the framework file and the announcement post, and the Internet Archive capture pages answer HTTP 403 here. Nothing was recorded from memory, so the two Meta editions carry no record from this pass. See retrievalFailures.
- **xAI Frontier Artificial Intelligence Framework, June 2026 edition: no record.** (frameworks). The dataset address returns HTTP 404 (NoSuchKey) and the Internet Archive capture answers HTTP 403 here. The December 2025 edition was retrieved and recorded; the comparison between the two editions, which the dataset only partly holds, is left open.
- **The D1 CBRN comparison cells (cmp-\*-d1-cbrn) and every biological provision already in data/claims.json.** (frameworks). Already in the dataset, as scope.md requires. Where a record adds to one, the dataset claim is named in `extends`: cmp-rsp-2023-d1-cbrn, prov-rsp-v2-0-misuse-uplift-threshold, rsp-v2-2-insider-scope, provchange-rsp-v3-3-novel-cb-threshold, cmp-fsf-2024-d1-cbrn, cmp-fsf-v2-0-d1-cbrn, fsf-v3-1-cbrn-tcls, cmp-preparedness-beta-2023-d1-cbrn, cmp-preparedness-v2-d1-cbrn, cmp-frontier-governance-2026-d1-cbrn, cmp-faif-2025-d1-cbrn, prov-faif-2026-heightened-safeguards and prov-faif-2025-bio-chem-acceptance-criterion.
- **The rationale and example columns of the capability tables of the Frontier Safety Framework v1.0, the Preparedness Framework Beta and Version 2, and the Frontier Governance Framework of May 2026.** (frameworks). Hazard-safe register. These columns descend to a technical or procedural level, and two of them name an agent class. Only the capability-level, threshold and tier columns were recorded, and each affected record carries a hazardNote.
- **The enumerated restricted critical steps and the threat-model stages of the xAI Frontier Artificial Intelligence Framework, December 2025, and the benchmark descriptions on its PDF p. 4.** (frameworks). Hazard-safe register. Only the heading sentence that states the policy was recorded (ab-fw-faif-2025-bio-chem-critical-steps). The named control lists, agent traits and stages of the threat model were read and excluded.
- **The passage in the Responsible Scaling Policy v1.0 (PDF p. 12) stating that evaluations for capabilities related to biological risks were previously carried out on a model similar to Claude 2 and showed it strictly below ASL-3.** (frameworks). It is a stated evaluation result rather than a provision, so it belongs to the `evaluations` pass, where it can carry attributes.evaluation with the report’s own conditions. Flagged here so that pass does not miss it.
- **The illustrative scorecard of the Preparedness Framework Beta (PDF pp. 15–16), which shows a CBRN row and a GPT-versus-search evaluation.** (frameworks). The edition marks the whole scorecard and the evaluation beside it as illustrative and says the specifics do not reflect results from real evaluations. Recording them as results would misstate the source; it also names an agent class, which is excluded.
- **The Anthropic analogy between AI Safety Levels and biosafety level standards (Responsible Scaling Policy v1.0, PDF p. 2).** (frameworks). It describes where the name of the scheme comes from, not a provision about biological risk, and no schema provision kind fits it. Worth a record if the maintainer wants the framing recorded; see openQuestions.
- **Per-edition restatements of provisions whose wording is identical across consecutive editions.** (frameworks). Bounded selection. The novel chemical/biological threat-pathway sharing provision, for example, is stated in the same words in Responsible Scaling Policy v3.0, v3.1, v3.2, v3.3 and v3.4; it is recorded for v3.0, with a quote, and for v3.2, so that the key is anchored in both the clean-text editions, but not for v3.1, v3.3 or v3.4. The off-cycle reporting trigger is recorded for v3.3, where it first appears, and for v3.4, the edition in force at the check date.
- **Executive Order 14110, subsection 4.4(b) (the nucleic acid synthesis screening framework and its implementing directives to NIST, funding agencies and DHS)** (measures). Assigned to this pack's synthesis screening pass, which covers the OSTP framework and the order's section that required it. Not recorded here to avoid a duplicate record of the same provisions.
- **Executive Order 14292 of 5 May 2026 (Improving the Safety and Security of Biological Research), whose Section 4(b) directs OSTP to revise or replace the 2024 Framework for Nucleic Acid Synthesis Screening** (measures). Retrieved and searched: the Federal Register text contains no occurrence of 'artificial intelligence'. It is a biological-research measure rather than an AI measure, and its screening provisions belong to the synthesis screening pass.
- **Executive Order 14110, subsection 4.1(a)(i)(C) (NIST initiative on guidance and benchmarks with a focus on cybersecurity and biosecurity) and subsection 4.4(a)(i)(B) (report to the President with recommendations on regulating or overseeing dual-use models)** (measures). Read and verified in the retrieved text but left out to keep the pass near its target size; both are noted in the qualifications of neighbouring records and can be added from the same cached source.
- **National security memorandum of 24 October 2024, subsections 3.3(g)(ii), (iii) and (v) (agency support for biosafety and biosecurity tools, agency uptake of AISI biological and chemical safety guidance, and in silico research guidance)** (measures). Read and verified; (ii) overlaps the synthesis screening pass (nucleic acid synthesis screening algorithms) and the others were dropped to keep the pass near its target size.
- **UK implementation report items on presenting papers to the Australia Group on AI risks and biosurveillance, and on an announced £115 million investment covering AI threats and biological security** (measures). Verified in the retrieved text; left out to keep the pass near its target size. The investment item is a report's account of an earlier announcement, so it would need an announced-stage record and was judged the weaker of the two.
- **The same training-data screening provision (no sensitive data from high-risk fields such as nuclear, biological, chemical and missile weapons) in AI Safety Governance Framework 3.0, §3.1.3(b)** (measures). The dataset already records this provision for editions 1.0 and 2.0 (c2-governance-tc260-asgf-v1-training-data-cbrn, c2-governance-tc260-asgf-v2-training-data-cbrn). The 3.0 instance was located and could be admitted as a new edition's provision; it was left out as a near-duplicate of recorded text.
- **GB/T 45654-2025 (Basic security requirements for generative AI service) and other Chinese national standards' annexes** (measures). Only the catalogue entry of GB/T 45654-2025 is publicly served (as the dataset source records); the standard's text was not retrieved, so no passage on biological risk could be checked.
- **China's Global AI Governance Action Plan (July 2025) and the State Council's "AI plus" opinion (August 2025)** (measures). The gov.cn URL tried for the Action Plan returned HTTP 404 and no other reachable copy was located in this pass; cac.gov.cn and npc.gov.cn refuse connections from this environment. Whether either text names biological risk is unresolved.
- **Biological Weapons Convention documents of the Working Group sessions 1 to 8, the 2022 Ninth Review Conference and the 2023, 2024 and 2026 meetings** (measures). No full-text search of UN documents is reachable from this environment (digitallibrary.un.org answers HTTP 202 with an empty body), so documents can only be found by enumerating symbols. The 2024 and 2025 meeting document lists were read; only the 2025 meeting's papers were retrieved and searched. Side events on AI and biological risk at the 2026 Working Group session were seen on the meeting page but are not State Party documents and are not recorded.
- **US agency documents of the Department of Energy and HHS, and CAISI/UK AISI joint assessments** (measures). Searches of presidential documents and the NIST centre page were made; a systematic search of DOE and HHS publications for texts naming AI and biology was not, and published evaluations belong to this pack's evaluations pass.
- **Executive Order 14110, section 4.4(a) (the Department of Homeland Security and Department of Energy work on AI and CBRN risk, and the National Academies study on how AI can increase biosecurity risks) and section 4.4(b)(iv) (the Department of Homeland Security framework for structured evaluation and stress testing of procurement screening, and its annual report)** (screening). Biosecurity sections of the order that are not themselves synthesis screening rules. The brief assigns the order's biosecurity sections to the `measures` pass; only the three subsections that create the screening framework and its funding condition are recorded here, to avoid two passes recording the same provisions.
- **The AI Action Plan of July 2025 and its nucleic acid synthesis screening item** (screening). The plan is already a document in the dataset and the `measures` pass covers its biosecurity items.
- **The two HHS notices on the review and revision of the 2010 guidance (85 FR 52611 of 26 August 2020 and 85 FR 69630 of 3 November 2020)** (screening). Located through the Federal Register API and recited in the 2022 and 2023 notices, but not retrieved separately, so no record is made of them. The 2022 draft notice is recorded and states the comment period of more than 120 days that followed them.
- **All technical content of the instruments read: the 2023 HHS guidance's sequence screening methodology and the definitions that state screening criteria; the OSTP framework's screening windows, matching approach and the criteria that may inform which sequences are in scope; sections 6 and the appendices of the IGSC protocol; the UK guidance's screening methodology and its list of indicators of a suspicious transaction** (screening). Excluded under the pack's hazard-safe rule. Each affected record's hazardNote says what was excluded. No agent, sequence, method, protocol or acquisition route is recorded anywhere in this draft.
- **The minimum length of order subject to screening, and its dated reduction, stated in the 2023 HHS guidance, the OSTP framework and the IGSC protocol** (screening). The parameter is a technical screening criterion and is not recorded, which also leaves out the dates attached to it. For the maintainer: the IGSC protocol dates the transition to 24 October 2026 'to conform to the requirements of the U.S. OSTP Framework', while the framework itself dates its further practices to 13 October 2026. The discrepancy is recorded here, not as a claim.
- **The HHS companion guide and frequently asked questions, the 2026 'Summary-Updates' PDF served from the ASPR site, the IGSC third-party conformity assessment member guidance (2026), the NIST test dataset for assessing baseline screening, and the Laird et al. inter-tool analysis (2026)** (screening). Located on the ASPR and IGSC pages but not retrieved in this pass. The conformity assessment guidance and the inter-tool analysis are candidates for the `evaluations` pass or a later increment.
- **State legislation that addresses AI and CBRN risk (California SB 1047 and SB 53, New York, Michigan, Illinois)** (screening). Already recorded in the dataset and in the frameworks-to-law pack; nothing is re-recorded here.
- **Bills of the 119th Congress (2025-2026), including any reintroduction of the Securing Gene Synthesis Act, and H.R. 4704 of the 118th Congress** (screening). No full-text bill search was reachable from this environment (see retrieval failures), so bills were found only by checking candidate package identifiers against the official bill-status records. H.R. 4704 is named in the official status record for S. 2399 as a related bill but is not separately recorded. The pack's bill coverage is therefore explicitly partial and limited to the 118th Congress items found.
- **The NIST notice 'Safety Considerations for Chemical and/or Biological AI Models' (4 October 2024) and three Centers for Disease Control and Prevention Paperwork Reduction Act notices of 2024 whose text refers to the Framework for Nucleic Acid Synthesis Screening** (screening). Found through the Federal Register API during discovery. They are agency documents naming AI and biology together rather than screening rules, so they belong to the `measures` pass or a later increment.
- **Screening rules outside the United States and the United Kingdom (for example any European Union or Chinese measure on synthetic nucleic acid screening), and the Australia Group and Biological Weapons Convention material the UK guidance points to** (screening). Not searched in this pass. An omission is not a judgement of importance.
- **The IGSC's statements that its members together represent a majority of global commercial gene-length synthesis capacity, and its account of its own incorporation in 2015** (screening). Not synthesis-screening provisions. The capacity statement is the consortium's own and would need independent evidence before being recorded as anything but a statement.
